For Europe, the central challenge is not simply to accelerate AI adoption, but to turn widespread experimentation
into productive, inclusive and trusted use Curiosity is not the main constraint: Europeans have embraced AI almost as
widely as Americans, but the technology has made fewer inroads into the workplace, and workers are less prepared to
use it effectively. The priority is therefore to remove the barriers that prevent AI from translating into productivity gains

and broader economic benefits. This requires action on five fronts:
1. Move AI from private experimentation into productive workplace use.  General-purpose technologies like
AI generate productivity gains only after firms make large complementary investments. These investments
show up as costs today, dragging down earnings and productivity in the short run, the so-called productivity
J-curve. Because the costs are immediate while the benefits are delayed, private investment may fall short,

in particular for SMEs, which face disproportionately high costs for the same implementation effort⁸. Europe
should reduce these costs that are holding back implementation, especially for SMEs, through accelerated
depreciation or temporary tax credits for identifiable complementary investments such as software integration,
data infrastructure, cybersecurity and process redesign. Public support should be tied to actual deployment
in business processes and, where possible, measurable productivity or efficiency gains rather than generic AI

subscriptions.
2. Close the training gap by starting with employers. This is primarily a provision problem: many workers
receive no offer of AI training, while most of those offered it take it up or plan to do so. Employer-led training
should therefore be supported through training tax credits, public co-financing and individual learning accounts,
with a focus on occupation-specific skills and groups currently least served including women, mid-career workers,

the secondary-educated and employees in smaller firms. Europe already has useful instruments: Germany’s
“Qualifizierungschancengesetz” subsidizes training costs and, in some cases, wage costs for employees affected
by technological change, while the EU  encourages member states to develop individual learning accounts that
give workers portable training entitlements. Where firms receive public support for major AI investments, this
could be linked to a workforce-training plan.

3. Shift labor-market policy from protecting jobs to supporting transitions.  Because AI is likely to change tasks
and occupations more extensively than it eliminates jobs outright, governments need to identify adjustment
pressures before they translate into unemployment. Combining occupation-level AI-exposure indicators
with vacancy, wage and skill-demand data can help identify vulnerable workers and emerging employment
opportunities. Workers in exposed occupations should have access to retraining while still employed, stronger

job-matching services, mobility grants and temporary wage insurance where moving into a new role initially
means lower pay. Such measures must go beyond traditional active labor-market programs, whose short-term
effects are often limited and vary considerably across program types and participants. Better targeting is crucial:
Policy priorities:
adopting AI better, not just faster
⁸  For productivity discussion, see: Brynjolfsson, Rock & Syverson 2021. „The Productivity J-Curve: How Intangibles Complement General Purpose

Technologies,“ American Economic Journal: Macroeconomics. For SMEs discussion, see: OECD 2025, “ AI adoption by small and medium-sized enter -
prises”,

### Page 26

Allianz Research
26
programs that invest in human capital accumulation, such as classroom-based vocational training and on-the-
job traineeships, show large gains, in particular for the long-term unemployed, whose skills have eroded during
extended periods out of work, and for women⁹.  Recent evidence confirms that well-designed interventions
do indeed deliver: cost-benefit analyses show that such training schemes can be highly cost effective, and

programs combining part-time work placements with training and short-term community work placements for
the unemployed produce lasting earnings gains for participants¹⁰. The key lesson is therefore to replace broad,
expensive programs with measures that are tightly linked to real-time labor-market signals.
4. Make sure the productivity dividend is broadly shared. AI is likely to raise productivity but could also
shift national income from labor to capital, and if automation outruns capital accumulation, wages could fall

even while output rises¹¹. Policy should therefore address both labor income and household wealth. On the
income side, broader access to skills can help translate productivity gains into better employment prospects
and wages. . On the asset side, funded pensions, employee share plans and wider household capital-market
participation can help more people benefit directly from AI-driven returns, particularly in Europe, where  equity
ownership is heavily concentrated among wealthier households¹². At the same time, the tax system should

avoid unnecessarily favoring capital over labor and adapt if a larger share of income shifts toward capital and
economic rents. Europe is particularly exposed here, as its tax systems rely more heavily on labor income taxes
and social contributions than the US, potentially putting greater pressure on public finances if the labor share of
income declines¹³.
5. Strengthen trust by targeting the risks people actually identify. Misinformation, data misuse and labor-

and human oversight and appeal rights for high-impact AI decisions. Rules should be credible and proportionate:
safeguards that build trust can support adoption, while complex or inconsistent requirements can slow diffusion,
especially for smaller firms. EU-wide templates, sector-specific guidance and regulatory sandboxes can help
make implementation more predictable without weakening protections.

Economic Association.

The survey queried more than 8,000 respondents across eight countries: Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Poland, Spain,
the United Kingdom and the United States. It was conducted as a web-based survey between 22 April and 25 May 2026.
Overall responsibility lies with Allianz Research, Allianz Investment Management SE. Sample planning and fieldwork

Respondents were selected using representative quota sampling. Qualtrics was given quotas specifying how many
people to survey in each country and which selection criteria to apply, with quotas set in line with official statistics for
gender and age groups. A comparison with official statistics confirms that the resulting sample broadly corresponds to
the population aged 18 and over in each of the eight countries.

Document 230: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

### Page 1

13023135     1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations

### Page 2

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

### Page 1

EQUITY: TECHNOLOGY
Genius Electronic Optical   3406.TW 3406 TT
Global Markets Research
5 October 2026
EBO and CPO an ideal leverage of its capabilities 
Quantifying EBO and CPO opportunities; reiterate Buy
Action: Maintain Buy; raise TP to TWD1,415, implying ~34% upside
We expect AI optical interconnect and communication opportunities (e.g. co-packaged optics 
fiber array unit [CPO FAU], expanded beam optical [EBO], micro multi-fiber connector [MMC]) 
to transform the traditional optical lens industry over the next five years, and believe 
Taiwanese handset camera lens makers (e.g. Largan [3008 TT, Buy] and Genius) possess 
inherent advantages to cater to these new markets, given their high-precision molding+plastic 
injection, optical design, active alignment, optical coating and optical inspection capabilities, as 
well as clean-room facilities and sufficient free cash flow from cash-cow handset lens 
businesses.
In addition to holding the position of Apple’s (AAPL US, Not rated) No. 2 lens supplier, we believe 
Genius has obtained 3M’s (MMM US, Not rated) EBO ferrule order and will start shipping by end-
2026F or early-2027F. Genius appears well-positioned to supply various CPO FAU 
components (lens, prism, V groove, FA, etc.) and may potentially handle FAU assembly if 
requested by customers. We estimate the EBO and CPO-related new businesses will 
contribute <3% and 16-20% earnings for Genius in 2027F and 2028F, respectively (details 
in Fig. 10). Yet, given the high dollar content and long-term growth potential of CPO/FAU related 
products over the next five years, we believe optical lens makers with inherent advantages in this 
new field deserve stock re-rating; we thus raise our target P/E to 25x (near the high end of the 
historical range of 10-30x since 2020), from 17x. We reiterate Buy, and raise our TP to 
TWD1,415, based on 25x 2028F EPS of TWD56.61 (vs. prior TP of TWD568, based on 17x 
2025F EPS of TWD33.4, set two years ago). Genius trades at 25x 2027F P/E.
Apple: Sunny’s market share loss and continued camera upgrades in 2027-28F
Sunny (2382 HK, Neutral) earlier faced some yield issues with the new variable aperture (VA) 
main camera lens for the iPhone 18 Pro series, and this could benefit Largan (more directly) and 
Genius (indirectly) in the competitive landscape. It remains uncertain whether Genius will be 
introduced as a third lens supplier to iPhone Pro’ main camera in the future, and this poses an 
upside risk to our forecasts. However, given the headwinds of rising memory costs and the 
potential cannibalization of the 18 Pro series by the iPhone Duo, we forecast slower iPhone lens 
shipments from end-2026F to 1H27F, before the 20th anniversary iPhone (Pro Max) and the 
second-gen iPhone Duo are introduced in 2H27F. We expect further upgrades to the iPhone Pro 
main camera in 2H27F, and introduction of a new periscope camera for the Pro Max in 2H28F.

Genius is the second source in Apple's lenses, and is gaining share in recent years. VR is a new growth engine for the company, with Facebook and

### Page 4

Optical communication – an ideal leverage of 
lens makers’ capabilities
Optical interconnect – from MPO to MMC and EBO
Multi-fiber push-on (MPO) is the current standardized physical-contact optical 
interconnect architecture designed to aggregate high-density ribbon fibers (typically 8, 12, 
16, 24 or 32 cores per ferrule) into a single compact interface. MPO connectors leverage 
precise physical contact enforced by a high mechanical spring force (around 10-20N) and 
steel guide pins for alignment to achieve ultra-low insertion loss (0.20-0.35dB) across 
clean fiber end-faces.
The micro multi-fiber connector (MMC) is another proprietary physical-contact optical 
interconnect standard beyond MPO developed by US Conec (unlisted) for high-density 
AI interconnects to directly address the server faceplate and panel space constraints. The 
MMC introduces a very small form-factor (VSFF) multi-fiber design that utilizes a 
miniaturized TMT ferrule (patented by US Conec; Fig. 1 ), and according to US Conec, 
the width of the TMT ferrule is similar to that of conventional MT ferrule yet with ~30% 
smaller in thickness and ~50% shorter in length to shrink the physical footprint to roughly 
one-third the size of an MPO. The spatial reduction consequently unlocks up to a 3x 
density advantage in patch panel ports and transceiver faceplates compared to standard 
MPO footprints (Fig. 2 ). Meanwhile, the MMC delivers a crucial density jump while 
preserving high optical performance, offering low insertion loss and supporting up to 
250um pitch multi-fiber ribbon arrays (including 12-, 16-, 24-, and 32-fiber configurations) 
to ensure interoperability with MPO ecosystems. The MMC also improves operational 
serviceability through US Conec’s DirectConec push-pull latching mechanism to allow 
technicians to plus and unplug individual cables even at a highly populated panel without 
external retraction tools.
Fig. 1:  TMT ferrule vs. conventional MT ferrule

Source: US Conec, Nomura research
Fig. 2:  MMC has a smaller footprint than MPO to enable a 3X 
optical interconnect density

Source: US Conec, Nomura research
Although MPO and MMC are the most popular standards of optical fiber connectors 
nowadays, they both rely on direct physical contact between ferrules and thus require 
higher spring pressure and precision alignments when connecting the cables. Also, 
physical contacts leave MPO and MMC’s exposed optical interfaces inherently 
susceptible to wear and tear over repeating mating cycles as well as potential signal 
degradation from dust and debris contamination (and therefore needs frequent cleaning 
and inspection).
Expanded beam optical (EBO), a patented technology by 3M (MMM US, Not rated; 3M 
owns over 100 patents on it, with 50+ patents pending), reshapes optical interconnect 
technologies by addressing pre-mentioned challenges of MPO or MMC, since EBO 
replaces traditional physical-contact end-face matings with non-contact, lens-based 
beam expansion (Fig. 4 ). EBO utilizes integrated total internal reflection (TIR) mirrors 
within precision polymer ferrules. As the light exits an optical fiber core and enters the 
ferrule, it strikes the internal TIR mirror that collimates and expands light beams up to 10x 
their core diameter before travelling through an intentional, tiny air gap to the mating 
Nomura | Genius Electronic Optical 5 October 2026
4

### Page 5

connector. An identical, paired TIR mirror is integrated within the receiving ferrule to 
capture the expanded beam and focuses it back into the receiving fiber core. According to 
3M, the EBO ferrule is vapor-coated with a special anti-reflection coating that efficiently 
reduces the reflection of light beams (Fig. 5 ).
Fig. 3:  An EBO ferrule

Source: 3M, Nomura research
Fig. 4:  Connection of two EBO ferrules

Source: Rosenberger, Nomura research

Fig. 5:  A cross-section illustration of EBO

Source: 3M, EBO MSA, Nomura research

Although one of the underlying issues with EBO is a higher insertion loss (0.35-0.75dB), 
3M’s EBO connector system still provides a few advantages compared to traditional 
physical-contact connectors:
Physical-contact connectors require frequent inspection and cleaning cycle before 
mating, while EBO is nearly immune to airborne particle contamination thanks to the 
embedded lens system and no direct surface contacts. An easier handling with 
reduced maintenance efforts therefore significantly lowers the installation time (as 
much as by 85%, according to 3M) and labor cost.
•
EBO can ensure consistent signal integrity across thousands of mating cycles due 
to contact-less designs to avoid face-wear and micro-scratching. By contrast, physical 
connectors grind against each other and ultimately lacks long-term durability.
•
Traditional connectors require precise fiber-to-fiber alignments, whereas the EBO 
connector system can be used in a hermaphroditic condition (socket/connector 
agnostic) given identical connector at both ends. As such, the risk of installation 
error is largely eliminated, even without the need for fiber-optic specialists.
•
EBO only demand a fraction (e.g. one-tenth) of the high spring pressure that 
compresses glass end-faces together in physical connectors, thereby minimizing 
•
Nomura | Genius Electronic Optical 5 October 2026
5

### Page 6

mechanical strains on chassis frames (demo video by 3M ). 
The contact-less, high density, blind mating, and low spring pressure features 
make EBO potentially an ideal optical connector for optical back-plane interconnect 
applications (similar to the function of orthogonal backplane or mid-plane in nVidia’s 
[NVDA US, Not rated] Kyber architecture), in our view.  
•
Fig. 6:  3M's current EBO ferrule offerings (12F/16F)

The EBO Multi-Source Agreement (EBO MSA) was established in March 2026 to turn 
3M’s proprietary EBO technology into an open, industry-wide standard for AI cluster 
infrastructure. The primary objective is to standardize EBO connector mechanical form 
factors, latching mechanisms, and optical fiber interface dimensions to allow hyperscalers 
to source interchangeable EBO connectors from multiple vendors rather than relying on 
one single supplier. According to EBO MSA, there are 9 end-user companies (including 
Microsoft [MSFT US, Not rated], Meta [META US, Not rated], nVidia, AMD [AMD US, Not 
rated], etc.) and 48 vendors in the membership by the end of September 2026 (Fig. 7 ). 
Members vote on specific connectors to develop and contribute design specifications – 
there are 18 types under discussion, and four are on the roadmap:
128f (8x16f) design specification is underway.•
16f MPO design is under discussion on specification.•
16f VSFF and external laser small form factor pluggable (ELSFP) are planned, and 
we note that EBO development for ELSFP could be challenging since high-power 
laser could damage the AR coating and polymer ferrules.
•

Who are the potential adopters of EBO connectors?
Of course, all the key members of EBO MSA are potential users of EBO connectors. But 
Nomura | Genius Electronic Optical 5 October 2026
6

### Page 7

for more concrete industry developments, 3M and Microsoft announced strategic
partnership in July 2026 (link ), and Microsoft was the first announced hyperscale cloud
provider to deploy 3M EBO technology in its Azure cloud. Microsoft’s early use of EBO
technology has shown the potential to reduce network deployment timelines in certain
environments. The technology has also demonstrated strong signal performance in live
data center conditions, where dust exposure and routine handling are typical during

installation and maintenance. The technology will help Microsoft reduce the need for
frequent cleaning and inspection while supporting reliable optical performance in dense,
high-volume deployment environments.
Meta joined EBO MSA in May 2026, and from our industry checks, we think Meta is likely
evaluating EBO technology for future ASIC mass-volume optical interconnect demand.
We also observe that AWS (AMZN US, Not rated), although it is not yet an MSA member,

could potentially evaluate this technology for its future ASIC mass-volume optical
interconnect demand.
Why optical plastic lens companies have advantages in
producing ferrules for passive optical connectors?
From MPO to MMC, the fiber density increases to 3x, and we notice that the precision
level requirements for MMC TMT ferrules also increase substantially versus MPO MT
ferrules. The high precision levels (<5um, or even <3um) of MMC TMT ferrules

usually exceed the traditional optical connector makers’ plastic injection capability
(usually >10um) and traditional connector makers need to invest new, higher precision
level tools to deal with those higher precision requirements. However, those precision
levels are about the right levels (or even lower-end than) which plastic camera lens
makers can do without much new investment in machine tools. This is why we think
plastic lens makers have advantage in the advanced passive optical connectors. The

reason why we do not see many lens makers enter MMC ferrule market at this moment is
because it is a patented technology by US Conec, and US Conec only licenses to limited
suppliers, such as Sumitomo (5802 JP, Buy), etc.
For EBO as well, although EBO ferrule looks complex in design and shape, it is a suitable
product for plastic camera lens makers to do. We estimate the difficulty and price of
EBO ferrule is around the levels of mid-range handset camera lenses.

3M likely outsources EBO ferrule to Genius
From our industry survey, 3M will likely outsource the EBO ferrule components to Genius,
which is done by high-precision plastic injection. Genius can leverage its handset camera
lens equipment and make new molds to produce the product. Hence, we do not expect
too much extra capex is required.
In our understanding, fiber connector makers can produce EBO after getting authorized
by 3M. We suspect that 3M will supply the key EBO ferrule to the authorized

manufacturers at least in the early stage of technology development. This may explain
why 3M comes to reach handset lens makers for the ferrule production.
Our base assumption for Genius’ EBO ferrule revenue contribution is about 2-3%
for 2027F and 2028F, by assuming an ASP of USD0.5-1 with 20-35mn units annual
volume. We assume the GM for this new product to be higher than corporate average.
The upside of EBO market will depend on the adoption and penetration of the technology

into hyperscalers’ AI optical interconnect roadmap. By our estimate, every rack
(containing 60-80 units of GPUs or AI ASICs), if adopting EBO for optical scale-up for
intra-rack or cross-rack interconnect technology, will potentially require 600-1,000
units of EBO ferrule components (300-500 pairs). Our base volume assumption for
Genius’ EBO shipments require one major CSP to adopt EBO in one major AI
platform, plus global sampling, yield loss, trial run demand and necessary

inventory buffer. If there are more than one major AI projects adopt EBO in 2028F, it will
represent potential upside to our forecasts.
CPO opportunities could be bigger than EBO, but less clear at
this moment
Unlike that EBO ferrule is a confirmed new business for Genius (though the market TAM
upside is unclear at this moment), CPO opportunities to Genius remain uncertain in
details, such as what products to offer, what are the business models, and who are

intermediate customers and/or end customers. Not to mention, the ramp-up of CPO
Nomura | Genius Electronic Optical 5 October 2026
7

### Page 8

market itself also has high uncertainties.
We believe Genius is working with FOCI (3363 TT, Not rated) in supplying key 
components for CPO FAU, such as V groove, prism, and/or fiber array (FA). It is also 
likely in talk with other FAU makers for various component supply (including lens, V 
groove, prism, FA, etc.). These FAU makers tend to do FAU final assembly in-house, and 
hence, may treat Genius as a component supplier to fill its internal capability or 
outsourcing partners’ gap. Genius’ flexibility in various component offering for CPO FAU 
could make it an easier partner for FAU customers, versus Largan (it tends to do more FA 
or FAU, rather than just components), and may see revenue contribution earlier than 
Largan. However, we assume USD25-35 ASP for Genius’ CPO FAU component offering 
in our base assumptions, given that it may supply more components rather than doing 
finished FAU products (ASP USD>100).
In the other side, we also see opportunities for Genius to get FAU module business from 
customers like Corning (GLW US, Not rated), but the progress of Genius to do such full 
assembly lines could be slower than Largan, as Genius still relies more on external 
equipment supply, unlike Largan having strong internal equipment design and 
manufacturing capability.
We assume CPO FAU to contribute Genius’ EPS by <1% in 2027F and 17% in 2028F.

Definition of Nomura Group's equity research rating system and sectors
The rating system is a relative system, indicating expected performance against a specific benchmark identified for each individual stock, subject to 
limited management discretion. An analyst’s target price is an assessment of the current intrinsic fair value of the stock based on an appropriate 
valuation methodology determined by the analyst. Valuation methodologies include, but are not limited to, discounted cash flow analysis, expected 
return on equity and multiple analysis. Analysts may also indicate expected absolute upside/downside relative to the stated target price, defined as

SECTORS
A 'Bullish' stance, indicates that the analyst expects the sector to outperform the Benchmark during the next 12 months. A 'Neutral' stance, 
indicates that the analyst expects the sector to perform in line with the Benchmark during the next 12 months. A 'Bearish' stance, indicates that the 
analyst expects the sector to underperform the Benchmark during the next 12 months. Sectors that are labelled as 'Not rated' or shown as 'N/A' are 
not assigned ratings. Benchmarks are as follows: United States: S&P 500; Europe: Dow Jones STOXX 600; Global Emerging Markets (ex-Asia): 
MSCI Emerging Markets ex-Asia. Japan/Asia ex-Japan: Sector ratings are not assigned.

Target Price
A Target Price, if discussed, indicates the analyst’s forecast for the share price with a 12-month time horizon, reflecting in part the analyst's 
estimates for the company's earnings. The achievement of any target price may be impeded by general market and macroeconomic trends, and by 
other risks related to the company or the market, and may not occur if the company's earnings differ from estimates.

GROWTH MOMENTUM TO SUSTAIN, 
EXTERNAL RISKS LINGER

India registered  strong growth at the start of 
FY27. Sustaining this performance will depend 
increasingly on how effectively it navigates a 
more uncertain global environment. Besides 
trade fragmentation, geopolitical tensions 
leading to higher energy prices, and shifts in 
international capital flows present key external 
risks.

India joined its Southeast Asian peers in 
posting an upside surprise on growth in 
1QFY27 (Apr -Jun26), up a firm 7.8%  yoy. few 
points of note:  
A) strong 1Q growth print reinforced the view 
that India’s domestic demand cycle was more 
resilient than initially assumed, with 
consumption, public capex and manufacturing 
providing a solid base despite external shocks.  
B) prevailing tailwinds by way of indirect tax 
relief in 4Q25, real rate buffer, and easier 
monetary conditions (earlier cuts), were also 
supportive;  
C) nominal GDP growth returned above 10% 
after two years on higher deflators, though 
some segments like manufacturing faced 
negative deflators as input prices rose at a 
faster pace than output due to commodity price 
increases and import intensity;  
D) Revisions to backdated numbers, to account 
for output PPI series, pushed up FY26 growth to 
7.8% (from 7.7% earlier). Two prior years were 
also revised.

www.dbs.com/insightsdirect/macro groupresc@dbs.com 
• High frequency indicators 
might keep  2QFY growth 
close to 7%. 
• Pace to moderate in 2HFY. 
• Retail inflation is likely to 
continue ris e and broaden  in 
scope, necessitating a tighter 
policy bias.  WPI likely to r ise 
by double-digits. 
• Special swap windows helped 
to stem bearish INR 
positioning. 
• Organic capital flows need to 
catch up.  
• Liquidity management is in 
focus, with a moderate 
correlation to inflation. 
• Twin-deficit risks are partly 
offset by stronger macro 
buffers.

### Page 2

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            2

High-frequency indicators

Into July -August, high frequency 
indicators are holding up, including GST 
e-way bills, indirect tax  revenues, 
electricity demand and growth in digital 
payments, while toll collections and 
petroleum consumption slipped . For 
demand, a contained extent of increase 
in retail pump prices shielded 
households from the surge in global 
crude prices, but other market linked 
fuel variants have climbed in recent 
months. On the employment front, job 
reductions were witnessed in the 
software sector, even as fresh 
opportunities were reported in global 
capability centres , electr onics/ 
semiconductors, healthcare, auto/ 
ancillary etc.  Wage growth in services 
has improved , manufacturing has been 
steady, while farm sector moderated, 
according t o consumption surveys by 
CMIE.

Industry indicators are a mixed bag, with 
PMIs maintaining their foothold in the 
expansionary terrain, while production 
and eight core index growth come off 
highs. Auto production continues to 
expand, though output and sales might 
moderate in the next quarter on base 
effects due to indirect tax cuts in the 
same time year ago. Forward-looking 
firm surveys have improved, after 
initially coming under pressure.

Sectors associated with rural demand  
might moderate amid weaker kharif 
sowing activity and lower reservoir 
levels impacting the rabi crop activity. 
Cumulative southwest monsoon is ~15% 
below the long-term average by tail end 
of the season. This takes the country on 
course to face its driest monsoon since 
2009 (which was 18% below LPA). 
Uneven geographical spread is notable, 
with south and east India registering 
nearly 25% shortfall.  While k harif 
sowing is on track with 99% of acreage 
completed, the winter crop might come 
under pressure as weather 
developments suggest El Niño  could 
extend into 2027.

### Page 3

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            3

With nominal GDP growth to track 11-12% 
this fiscal yea r, corporate earning s 
growth is expected to fare better than 
recent few years. 1QFY27 performance 
had already set off the year on a strong 
note, barring selected sectors like O&G 
which faced sequential marginal 
pressures from operating costs. Add to 
this, the centre’s capex gr owth is in firm 
double-digits, also backed by growth in 
core imports (non-oil non-gold).

On the external sector , despite better 
performance by exports, oil and 
electronics purchases have weighed on 
the goods math. The trade deficit is set 
to stay wide amidst elevated global 
crude cost, higher industrial fuels, 
cooking gas etc,  even as a wider mix of 
suppliers have addressed energy 
shortages. Notably, the net electronics 
deficit has widened considerably, just 
as exports have improved. Likewise, core 
imports (ex oil ex gold) have stayed firm, 
which partly feeds into higher exports as 
well as broader domestic demand. 
Monthly service trade surplus has been 
rangebound around $17 -18bn in recent 
months dwarfed by $25 -$30bn goods 
gap.

Maintaining growth forecasts

High frequency indicators put 2QFY 
growth close to 7%. We expect full year 
FY27 growth to average 7.3% yoy  from 
a revised 7.8% in FY26 , assuming 
moderation in the momentum in the 
second half of the year on tighter policy 
conditions, lagged impact of high energy 
prices and base effects from indirect tax 
cuts last year fade.  Assuming some 
spillover impact of exogenous 
uncertainties and tight financial 
conditions at home, we expect FY28 
growth to average 6.8-7.0%.

INFLATION OFF ITS BACK – 
THREE CATALYSTS

After two years of benign inflation, 
India’s price trend is off its back this 
year, mainly on the back of three

### Page 4

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            4

catalysts: Food, energy and early signs 
of demand pass through.

Aug inflation rose to a 20 -month high of 
4.8% yoy from 4.5% month before . As 
the chart highlights, number of sub -
segments growing by faster than the 
inflation target at 4% have continued to 
climb, pointing to a broadening out in the 
momentum.

Food inflation is gradually becoming 
more broad -based, shifting beyond 
vegetables and pulses to sugar, milk, 
protein groups, edible oils, amongst 
others. Sub-par weather has contributed 
to this upmove, besides idiosyncratic 
factors like swing in production  trends, 
diversion for fuel usage etc. After two 
years of normal monsoon, cumulative 
southwest rainfall this year is currently 
15% below the long -term average, 
alongside slower build -up in reservoir 
levels, which could impact upcoming rabi 
crops as well.  El-Nino patterns continue 
to strengthen, adding to seasonal swings 
and risk of impacting farm output.

Beyond food, g lobal oil prices have 
averaged ~$91pb in CY26, staying above 
$100pb for much of Seo26 . Retail fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
back in May26 and have been left 
unchanged since then , while CNG, 
aviation fuel, LPG etc have  continued to 
climb.

Transport inflation jumped to 4.6% yoy in 
Aug vs 0.1% in Jan26, besides utilities’ 
component inching up. Business price 
expectation surveys (conducted by IIM 
Ahmedabad) have, nonetheless, 
captured businesses facing a notable 
increase in fuel costs, especially 
industrial fuels, base metals and 
derivative product groups. Imported 
price pressures are most notabl e in the 
producer price/ wholesale price indices. 
These gauges have been hovering at 9 -
10% yoy in recent months.

### Page 5

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            5

Lastly, core inflation  (ex-food and fuel)  
rose to 1.5-month high in August, ma inly 
due to a sequential rebound in precious 
metals – gold and silver jewellery. 
Excluding precious metals, the reading 
was more moderate at 2.9%, pointing to 
some extent of economic slack. 
Nonetheless, other pockets like telecom, 
restaurants, business services, transport 
fares etc have been inching up, 
reflecting early signs of broadening in 
price pressures (on sequential basis), 
warranting attention. These are set 
against the backdrop of strong bank 
credit growth, flush INR liquidity and 
resilience in headline growth.

Our correlation analysis between 
liquidity (as % of NDTL) and core inflation 
– see chart, points to a positive but 
modest corelation at 0.4 between the 
two variables, with an assumed year -
worth lag on inflation. The inflation -
fighting mandate will also pus h the 
central bank to reduce the surplus 
liquidity in the domestic system.

A sh arp in global commodity prices is 
expected to fe ed into the wholesale 
price gauge , pushing up reading s to 
double-digits next quarter.

A gradual broadening of price 
pressures is likely to keep headline 
inflation above 5% in second half of the 
fiscal year , sharply narrowing the real 
rate buffer, underscoring the need for a 
tighter policy bias. Add to this, recent 
developments, including a sustained rise 
in crude prices, tightening global 
financial conditions, firm domestic 
growth and signs of broadening in core 
pressures, strengthen the case for a  
shallow 50bp hike in second half of FY27, 
making October’s meeting a live one.

TACKLING LIQUIDITY DELUGE, 
HIGHER YIELDS

Beating street estimates by a wide 
margin, the RBI’s special swap windows 
raised a cumulative $ 143bn into Sep , 
with the highest contribution (~92%) by

### Page 6

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            6

FCNR(B) deposits at $1 33bn, while rest 
were via offshore borrowing facilities. 
These flows pushed up the foreign 
reserves stock to a fresh high past 
$780bn, providing considerable 
firepower to defend the currency. This is 
already noticeable in the recent shift in 
the intervention bias. USDINR, which was 
earlier stubbornly steady in the face of 
strong FCNR inflows but  eased sharply 
due to strong intervention dollar sales 
and broader dollar swings, before being 
led higher due to external triggers.

Liquidity is a key watch factor. Given the 
swap arrangement, these inflows have 
added to already abundant INR liquidity 
backdrop, which was at a four year high, 
depressing overnight rates. The banking 
system surplus jumped past INR 10trn 
surpassing 2022 and Covid highs, with 
the durable balance widening sharply 
higher to INR 14trn, suppressing 
overnight call rates. This compares to 
average INR 1trn surplus in late-June.

While organic drivers like tax -related 
outflows, and seasonal currency leakage, 
in addition to CAD, portfolio outflows, 
and maturity of the forwards book will 
act as counter -balancing factors, yet 
concerted steps were required to drain 
the potential surge in liquidity.

We discussed the various seasonal, 
near-term and permanent measures  
that the central bank might consider (see 
FCNR bonanza necessitates deft 
management) to absorb this surfeit. 
Policymakers are being mindful of both 
the liquidity and signalling effects of 
the sterilisation tools at their disposal. 
The RBI utilised tranches of market 
operations like VRRR across tenors, 
after which three tranches of open 
market operations (bonds maturing FY29 
and FY32) worth a cumulative INR 1trn 
were conducted. These announcements 
fuelled a rise in yields, which were 
already inching up, tracking global 
counterparts. Sell/buy FX swaps for  
shorter rollovers were also being 
reportedly tapped.

When the dust settles, focus will also 
be on the bunched -up maturities that 
will fall due in 3Y and 5Y tenor of the 
deposits. A portion of the existing 
reserve stock could be earmarked 
against these liabilities, helping to 
mitigate concerns that deposit 
maturities or debt repayments could 
trigger a sharp increase in dollar demand 
and exert pressure on the FX market 
down the line. In the near-term, priorities 
will be to manage liquidity, gradually 
lower the sizeable forwards book, and 
support the domestic currency. Long -
end yields continue to be influenced by 
the hardening global yields.

BALANCE OF PAYMENTS MATH 
TO BE COMFORTABLE

The FY27 current account math is 
expected to display two key counter 
trends, Firstly, the wider goods trade 
imbalance will raise the FY27 current 
account deficit. Secondly, the 
counterbalancing effect of a sharp 
improvement in the capital flows math 
will keep the annual balance of 
payments math in surplus.

### Page 7

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            7

India recorded a current account deficit 
(CAD) of $10.5bn during April-July 2026, 
compared with $6.1bn in the 
corresponding period last year. The 
deterioration was driven almost entirely 
by a wider merchandise trade deficit, 
which rose to $117.8bn from $97.1bn a 
year ago. Part of this offset by higher  
income, transfers and capital account 
flow. Capital flows found support from 
net FDI, portfolio investments and 
banking capital, especially NRI deposits 
(jumped sharply to $36bn vs $4.6bn 
same period last year) , pushing up Apr -
Jul26 BOP to $12.7bn surplus, more than 
double of $4.8bn same time last year.

Oil prices are the main swing factor for 
the CAD this year , with the current run -
rate tracking our forecast at -1.1% of GDP 
for FY27. This suggests that the 
widening in the CAD is commodity -price 
related rather than driven by higher 
demand.

On the other hand, capital flows are 
being buttressed by the special swap 
windows introduced by the central bank, 
which raised a cumulative $ 143bn 
between June and August, through non -
resident deposits and external 
borrowings. This has helped to fil l the 
gap from subdued portfolio flows and 
net investment trends. We expect FY27 
current account deficit to track -1.1% of 
GDP in FY27, with the balance of 
payments to register upwards of $80bn 
surplus (vs -$23.6bn deficit last year).  
Into FY28, the dependency of capital 
flows on o rganic drivers like portfolio 
flows and FDI is set to increase, 
underscoring the need for higher 
vigilance.

FISCAL RUN-RATE

The FY27 fiscal run -rate points to a 
stronger revenue momentum, which 
has helped to keep the FYTD deficit in 
check, even as expenditure was 
frontloaded owing to subsidy payments 
and capex disbursements. April-July 
2026 deficit stands at a third of the full-
year balance.

### Page 8

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            8

Both tax and non -tax receipts fared 
better than the comparable period year 
ago, helped by stronger corporate tax 
collections, STT transaction revenues, 
and RBI dividend, amongst others.

Setting 2QFY for a strong start, net 
direct tax collections r ose 13% yoy by 
mid-Sep26, driven by net corporate tax 
collections up 19.5%. Net non -corporate 
tax collections were up 6%, besides STT 
collections up a sharp 53% yoy. Add to 
this, divestments have also fared better 
than expected this year, with ~78% 
raised by August, accompanied by 
dividends & profits (at 83% of budget by 
Jul26).

Under expenditure, about 33% of the full 
year allocation was allocated by July 
2026. The subsidy outgo rose sharply, up 
35% yoy, led by higher food at 23% and 
fertiliser at 46% (already accounting for 
nearly two-third of the full year budget)  
led by surging global DAP and urea 
prices. With global prices remaining high, 
the actual outgo might be higher than 
budgeted INR 1.7trn , likely towards INR 
2.5trn. Capital spending also remained 
apace, up 30% yoy in first five months of 
the year.

Overall, strong growth in tax and non-tax 
receipts allowed the centre to sustain 
higher spending while containing the 
extent of fiscal slippage until July 2026 
(FYTD). A strong nominal GDP rate 
(helped by higher inflation) will also 
provide a favourable denominator effect.

IN SUM

The economy exhibited resilience in the 
face of a tough external environment in 
the first quarter, with part of that 
stability likely to extend to 2QFY27 as 
well. Tighter monetary conditions, El 
Nino impact and base effects from 
passage of post -indirect tax jump will 
moderate headline growth in 2HFY. 
Nonetheless for the year, we expected 
growth to stay north of 7%, factoring in a 
strong start.

Oil prices remain well supported with plenty of risks still facing
Persian Gulf supply. The market, meanwhile, doesn’t seem to
be buying the Russia-Ukraine energy ceaseﬁre

Warren Patterson and Ewa Manthey
Energy – Scepticism over Russia-Ukraine
ceaseﬁre
The weakness in the oil market following President Trump’s
announcement of an energy ceaseﬁre between Russia and Ukraine
was short-lived. That goes, too, for the US-Russia diesel export deal.
ICE Brent settled 1.8% higher, just shy of $107/bbl yesterday — the
highest close since mid-September. Clearly, the market is more
concerned over recent developments in the Middle East, with Houthi
attacks on Saudi Arabia and the lingering risks to oil ﬂows from the
Persian Gulf. In addition, there’s considerable scepticism over the
Russia-Ukraine energy ceaseﬁre, particularly with reports that
Russia has hit Ukrainian energy targets since Trump announced it.
While diesel initially saw some weakness following the US-Russia

deal, strength has returned. The volumes suggested are doing little
to oﬀset the tightness in the global middle distillate market.
The Persian Gulf could see further bypass volumes in a relatively
short time period. The Saudi Energy Minister said that the country
is looking to expand its pipeline export capacity to 10m b/d, up
from a current 7m b/d. About 1.2m b/d of this expansion could
come from converting a gas pipeline to crude and could be
available to transport oil within 7-8 weeks. Expansion beyond this

would take more time, possibly as much as 3 years. Shipping larger
volumes from the Red Sea would also require further investment in
loading capacity at ports.
Meanwhile, hurricane activity in the Gulf of Mexico continues to
disrupt oﬀshore operations for US producers; they’ve shut in almost
605k b/d of oil production, which is equivalent to nearly 30% of
total US production in the Gulf of Mexico, according to the Marine
Minerals Administration. However, output has been recovering in

recent days, with as much as 1.5m b/d shut-in on Friday.
The European natural gas market continues to see stronger prices.
Front-month TTF futures settling above EUR84/MWh and trading to
its highest level since December 2022. The market is grappling with
the idea that we’re about to go through a large part of the Northern
hemisphere winter with little Persian Gulf LNG supply. This risks
fueling increased competition between Asian and European buyers

through the winter, leaving the market vulnerable to spikes higher.
In addition, we’re seeing mandated buying by some EU member
countries in an attempt to hit storage targets. Germany is only
adding additional support to European gas prices. EU gas storage is
just shy of 75% full, below the 83% seen at the same stage last
year and also below the 5-year average of 89%.
Metals - Tight supplies drive copper close to
record
LME copper rose for a second consecutive session, trading near

record highs. Supply concerns are adding support following a fatal
avalanche at Chile’s El Teniente mine on Sunday. It led to the
suspension of operations in aﬀected areas. While the open pit at

因矽中介層產能不⾜，台積電⾸次合作外包給競爭者 GFS ，這應該算
台積電的主動外溢訂單
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
OCT 9 ∙ PAID

READ IN APP
What is new?
半導體晶圓代⼯領域誕⽣了⼀項具備歷史分⽔嶺意義的競合⼤案，全球晶圓代
⼯⿓頭台積電（ TSMC ）與⻑期競爭對⼿美商格芯（ GlobalFoundries, GFS ）
正式簽署⼀項為期五年、價值  20 億美元， 2028 上半年開始量產的製造供應
協議。根據合約，格芯將在其位於美國紐約州⾺爾他（ Malta, NY ）的旗艦
Fab 8 晶圓廠，專⾨為台積電的  CoWoS 先進封裝⽣態系⽣產最關鍵的「矽中
介層（ Silicon Interposers ）」。 Silicon interposer 做完 RDL 層後與邏輯晶⽚
與記憶體進⾏接合，對⾼頻寬、訊號完整性與封裝良率⾄關重要；它不是最先
進  GPU 邏輯  die ，也不代表  GlobalFoundries 取得  Nvidia ∕ AMD 的先進
節點代⼯。
格芯因此成為全美國境內第⼀座具備量產能⼒、專為先進封裝供貨的本⼟矽中
介層供應基地，徹底解決了台積電亞利桑那鳳凰城  Fab 21 在美⽣產

4nm/3nm/2nm 裸晶後，因美國缺乏先進矽中介層製造⽽被迫將晶圓空運回
台灣封裝或將矽中介層空運到美國的荒謬地緣斷鏈難題。

美商格芯（ GlobalFoundries, GFS ）：我們估計台積電  20 億美元保證採

紐約  Fab 8 的成熟與特殊製程產能利⽤率，且無需承擔極紫外光（ EUV ）微縮
⾵險即可跨⼊⾼⽑利的  AI 先進封裝關鍵環節。但要是台積電在 2030 年⽤玻璃
中介層替代矽中介層轉進  CoPoS ，這訂單上修的機會將被移除。
台積電：以極低資本開⽀成功化解美國⽩宮與國防部對「晶⽚在美製造、封裝
在台完成」的強烈地緣政治施壓，建⽴完全在美閉環的  CoWoS 替代供應鏈
（台積電 4/3/2nm 邏輯晶⽚ +GFS 的 Silicon Interposer+Amkor 的  WoS 封

測），同時將台積電美國的產能完全留給最⾼附加價值的晶粒堆疊（ Chip-on-
Wafer ）。

Negatively aﬀect what supply chain vendors and
why?
台灣⼆線矽中介層代⼯廠（聯電  2303.TW 、世界先進  5347.TW ）：台積電
過去在台灣將部分  CoWoS-S 矽中介層外包給聯電等本⼟夥伴；隨著美系客戶
指定美國在地化⽣產，聯電爭取海外  CoWoS 衍⽣中介層訂單的空間遭到分
流，美國第⼆來源降低稀缺溢價並提⾼客戶議價權。
Our outside-the-box, non-consensus thinking
台積電把關鍵的  CoWoS 中介層交給技術競爭對⼿，是否引狼⼊室？我們的⾮

共識觀點指出，矽中介層本質上是⾼耗⾯積、低電晶體密度的低⽑利率材料。
矽中介層通常使⽤  65nm 甚⾄  90nm ⽼舊製程製造，上⾯幾乎沒有主動電晶
體，純粹是⽤深矽穿孔（ TSV ）進⾏⾼密度⾦屬布線  RDL ，但它卻要霸佔珍貴
的  12 吋潔淨室機台空間。台積電將這種「吃產能、賺⼯錢」的低⽑利環節甩
給急需產能利⽤率的格芯，⾃⼰則全⼒聚焦在單⽚要價  3 萬美元的
3nm/2nm 核⼼運算晶粒與頂層⾼難度鍵合。台積電不是出讓技術，⽽是進⾏
⾼超的微笑曲線資本利潤套利。
⽽且這筆交易的真正戰略價值不僅僅是美國製造，⽽是把先進封裝拆成可外包
的模組。若  TSMC 能標準化  interposer PDK 、測試與良率資料，未來可像前
段  foundry 的多廠調度⼀樣管理封裝供應鏈；這會提⾼總體產能韌性，但也
把競爭焦點轉到跨廠協同、良率  ownership 與責任界⾯。⻑期勝者可能不是
單⼀設備商，⽽是能把  design-to-package 數位線索串起來的  EDA ∕

manufacturing data 平台。
Our advice to investors
我們不該把協議解讀為  TSMC 技術外流或  GlobalFoundries 進⼊最先進邏輯
製程。應繼續追蹤四項指標： 1. 1H28 是否能實際量產、 2. GFS 的  silicon
interposer 良率與產能、 3. TSMC 內部  CoWoS 瓶頸釋放幅度、 4. 是否追加
訂單。 US2bn 若平均分五年為 US400mn ∕年，但估值模型應按  2028 起的爬
坡曲線認列。
這項世紀⼤和解是台積電進⾏地緣政治壓⼒卸載的操作。台積電不僅沒有因此
喪失封裝主導權，反⽽⽤外包中介層的代價，將原本對⽴的格芯轉化為⾃⾝的
利益同盟，並在美國本⼟牢牢築起  CoWoS ⽣態圈。我們維持對台積電⻑期的
正⾯評價並同時看好格芯（ GFS ）的結構性轉變。

Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• Consumer spending is holding up, and we expect real PCE to rise at around a 3.0% annualized rate
in Q3.
Consumer spending has been mixed, yet resilient in recent months. After a soft July, driven largely by

weaker durable goods purchases, retail sales rebounded strongly in August, rising at a 1.2% monthly
clip amid broadly distributed gains across retailers. The latest data have led us to lift our near-term
growth forecast, and we now look for real PCE to rise at a 3.2% annualized clip in Q3. Previous data
will also likely be revised higher on firmer control group sales data and strong services revenue in Q2,
suggesting recent consumer momentum remains intact. All told, the consumer is still in decent shape

and has capacity to keep spending. Real income growth remains modest but is beginning to improve
as inflation eases and the labor market remains broadly supportive suggesting continued consumption
even if that pace of spending may moderate into year-end.
Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build-out is still king in terms of the contribution to business investment, but the
broadening in capex spending beyond the tech sector is taking root.

AI-related investment continues to dominate capital allocation. There are no signs of firms scaling
back their outlays now, though at some point as AI investment matures, merely sustaining spending
at elevated levels will provide a smaller incremental boost to growth than it has in recent quarters.
Beyond AI, the tentative signs we've identified that signal a broadening in the investment backdrop
are getting a bit louder. The ISM manufacturing index has been in expansion territory for eight-

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
straight months, and durable goods orders have firmed across a wider range of industries. Part of
this improvement likely reflects delayed capital spending plans finally moving forward, but inventory
rebuilding after five consecutive quarters of drawdown may be lending some support as well.

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce and Wells Fargo Economics
Investment: Residential
• Residential construction looks likely to taper through the rest of the year while mortgage rates and
new home inventories remain elevated.
Headwinds have intensified against residential construction. The trend in single-family construction is
downshifting, emblematic of the affordability challenges depressing buyer demand. As of this writing,

mortgage rates have risen to over 7.0% and builder confidence has declined alongside. Sales stuck in
a low gear mean that new home listings are inching higher, further discouraging new development.
Multifamily construction appears to be holding up but faces limited potential upside with vacancy
rates elevated across much of the South. We still see a potential for marginal improvement in 2027,
but higher mortgage rates dampen our outlook.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures

• Construction appears to be gaining steam, led by data centers and power. However, sluggish
commercial starts elsewhere and high interest rates are likely to keep growth relatively modest.
The AI infrastructure build out is supporting modest growth in nonresidential construction. After
moderating for a full year, private nonresidential outlays rose from April to July. Data centers and
power are propelling current momentum. Though construction has also strengthened across sports

facilities and communication infrastructure, commercial projects remain stuck in low gear. Categories
like warehousing and retail remain soft, and overall architectural billings have improved only modestly.
Looking ahead, a recent breakout in megaprojects is likely to support additional data center and
manufacturing development, while higher interest rates limit overall growth.

Net Exports & Trade
• Net exports are tracking to slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth amid
strong imports.
Imports are shaping up for another strong quarter after a surge in July and a rebound in August
retail spending suggesting continued domestic demand for product. The July import gain was again

almost entirely driven by high-tech related goods like computers & accessories, semiconductors and
telecommunications equipment. We still anticipate a bit more of a broadening out in imports to come,
perhaps as early as August as businesses replenish inventory amid continued consumer demand.
Export growth is a bit more constrained, suggesting an expansion in the trade deficit and that net
exports will slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth in Q3.

Labor Market
• We modestly raised our 2026 payroll forecast and made no material changes to our 2027 forecast.
We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end this year at 4.2%.
August's employment report reinforced that the labor market remains stable. Payroll growth has
averaged 106K over the past six months, though we expect some moderation in the months ahead.
Hiring measures from regional Fed surveys and hiring plans from small businesses have cooled from

their early summer jumps and job openings have leveled off. At the same time, initial jobless claims
remain near multi-decade lows and continue to indicate that layoffs remain contained. We expect
payroll growth to moderate to roughly 65K per month over the remainder of the year, a pace broadly
consistent with our estimate of breakeven. As hiring cools slightly and the participation rate rebounds
somewhat further, we see some scope for the unemployment rate to edge up to 4.2% by year-end.

Even with the slight increase though, labor market conditions would remain consistent with full
employment.
Inflation
• Incorporating our expectations for the upcoming BEA methodology changes and recent inflation
data, we look for the core PCE deflator to end the year at 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis, up from
3.1% in our previous forecast.
Headline inflation remains tied to the conflict in the Middle East, although underlying price pressures
continue to move in a more favorable direction. Higher oil prices are keeping some upward pressure

on energy-sensitive categories in the near term, but tariff-related goods inflation is fading and price
pressures outside a handful of technology-related categories remain limited. Meanwhile, core services
inflation remains on a moderating trend as shelter inflation continues to gradually cool and labor cost
pressures slowly ease. Taken together, inflation pressures appear less widespread than earlier this year,
Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
but recurring supply shocks are likely to keep the path back to 2% bumpy. We expect the core PCE
deflator to slow to 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis this year.

Fiscal Policy
• We forecast this year's federal budget deficit will total $2.1 trillion (~6.5% of GDP), and next year's
will be about the same in dollar terms and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).

The federal budget deficit continues to hover in the 6.0-6.5% of GDP range, similar to where it was
at the end of the Biden administration. The outlook for the next couple of years is one of status quo.
We doubt there will be any new, big fiscal legislation with divided government likely after the midterm
elections, but rising interest costs and steady growth in mandatory spending programs such as Social
Security and Medicare will keep deficits from shrinking much. It's not just U.S. fiscal problems putting

upward pressure on long-term rates. Other factors such as robust corporate debt issuance, policy
uncertainty and foreign factors are also playing a role. Our base case for 6.0%-6.5% of GDP budget
deficits will not help when it comes to bringing down long-term interest rates.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

U.S. Economic Outlook: September 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• We have changed our fed funds forecast to 4.00-4.25% by year-end 2026, with no changes in
2027. Our year-end forecasts for the 10-year Treasury yield are also higher at 4.90% and 4.75%,
respectively, for this year and next.
The FOMC made clear at its September 16 meeting that stubbornly high PCE inflation this year was
the key factor behind the Committee's decision to raise rates. Given this, we think only a string of

good inflation data can bail the Fed out, and accordingly we think one more rate hike is coming this
year, with softer inflation data in 2027 giving way to a more patient FOMC. We could easily envision
one more hike beyond that or a "one and done" hike where the FOMC does not go again. Outcomes
outside that range, however, strike us as unlikely. A rising neutral rate makes meaningful rate cuts hard
to justify in the absence of an economic slowdown, while the case for a more aggressive tightening

cycle seems weak given that the labor market is not overheating and much of the excess inflation is
supply side driven. We think a very short gradual tightening cycle and an eventual reduction in energy
prices/slowdown in inflation will allow Treasury yields to drift modestly lower in 2027. But, the factors
mentioned above (e.g., large budget deficits, rising neutral rate) should prevent yields from falling in a
bigger way.

International Developments
• Global growth has proved more resilient than expected, prompting us to upgrade our 2026 global
GDP growth forecast to 2.9%. We have also modestly revised our global inflation outlook higher,
lifting our 2026 CPI forecast to 4.4% and our 2027 forecast accordingly.
• We now expect more tightening across advanced economies than in our previous monthly forecast
update, while emerging market policy paths remain mixed.

Global economic activity proved more resilient than expected in H1-2026, with early indicators
suggesting that momentum carried into Q3. While precautionary stockpiling supported some of the
earlier strength, activity has remained resilient on firmer services, continued AI-related investment and
exports (U.S., South Korea, Japan and Australia) and increased defense spending (Eurozone and Japan).
We expect inflation to remain higher for longer before easing next year and averaging 4% in 2027 as

tighter financial conditions weigh on demand and supply pressures fade. Risks remain skewed to the
upside. Oil and product inventories remain below pre-conflict levels, while limited refining capacity and
ongoing disruptions leave energy prices vulnerable to renewed pressure. El Niño and higher fertilizer
costs threaten food prices, while AI-related demand is raising semiconductor costs. Second-round
effects remain limited, but successive supply shocks increase the risk of persistent inflation into next

year.

Asia Trends

2026 年 10 月 2 日

1/2 
タイ、洪水被害が景気の新たな重石に 
～経済活動への影響が広がるなか、債務増大と金利高止まりが財政対応を制約～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ タイでは９月中旬以降、豪雨による洪水被害が全国77 県のうち 44 県に拡大している。2011
年の大洪水を受けて工業団地の浸水対策が進んだため、現時点で当時ほど深刻な被害は確認
されていないものの、一部工場の浸水や道路冠水による物流障害が発生し、自動車工場の操
業停止やタイ国際航空の貨物受付停止、欠航など経済活動への影響が広がっている。被害額
は 110 億～350 億バーツ （最大ＧＤＰ比0.2％） に達するとの見方もあり、 原油高や公共投資
の遅れを背景に景気が減速するタイ経済にさらなる下押し圧力がかかる懸念がある。 
○ 政府は洪水被災者支援に20 億バーツを充てるほか、2027 年度の新規借入枠を1.26 兆バーツ
とする公的債務管理計画を承認した。ただし、公的債務残高は 2027 年末にＧＤＰ比 69％と
法定上限の70％に迫る見通しであり、追加的な財政対応の余地は狭まりつつある。加えて、
原油高や食料供給不安を背景とするインフレ懸念、世界的な金利上昇圧力を受けてタイの長
期金利も高止まりしている。洪水対策など歳出圧力が高まるなか、財政余力の低下と債務負
担の増大が、先行きのタイ経済の足かせとなる可能性がある。

タイでは、９月中旬以降に豪雨による洪水被害が広がり、同国中部や首都バンコク周辺など、全
77 県のうち44 県が被災する事態に発展している。 同国では、2011 年に断続的な豪雨をきっかけに
河川が氾濫し、 中南部を中心とする工業団地が浸水被害に直面して操業停止に追い込まれた。 その
後、 主要な工業団地においては防潮堤などの浸水対策が進められた。 このため、 現時点では2011 年
のような深刻な浸水は確認されていない。 
しかし、 一部の工場が浸水被害に見舞われたほか、 周辺道路の冠水により物流に悪影響が出るな
ど、幅広い経済活動に支障が生じている。現地報道では、部品納入に支障が生じていることを理由
に、複数の自動車工場が操業停止に追い込まれている。タイ国際航空も、多くの従業員が出勤でき
ないことを理由に、当面の到着・出発貨物の受付を停止することを発表している。さらに、手荷物
の取り扱い業務の一部に深刻な影響が出たほか、機材繰りを理由に一部で欠航が生じている。 
洪水被害が一段と長期化する懸念があるものの、現時点において、洪水による被害額は少なくと
も 110 億バーツ、最大で350 億バーツ（ＧＤＰ比0.2％）に及ぶとの見方が出ている。足元のタイ

2/2 
経済は、 イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油高によるエネルギーインフレが個人消費の足かせとなると
ともに、公共投資の進捗の遅れも重なり、４-６月の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前期比年率▲0.74％と３
四半期ぶりのマイナス成長となるなど、 景気に急ブレーキがかかっている （注１）。こうしたなか、
当面のタイ経済を取り巻く環境は厳しさを増すことが避けられなくなっている。 
政府は５月、イラン情勢の悪化による原油高を受けたエネルギー構造改革を目的に、4,000 億バ
ーツを上限とする緊急借入枠を設ける
ことを承認した。 さらに、 今回の洪水被
害を受けて、 先月末には補正予算計画の
一環として、被災者支援を目的に 20 億
バーツを充てる方針を明らかにした。 加
えて、2027 年度の公的債務管理計画を承
認し、新規借入枠を 1.26 兆バーツとす
ることを決定した。 同計画では、2026 年
末の公的債務残高はＧＤＰ比68％、2027
年末には69％と一段と法定上限（70％）
に近付く。足元では公的債務残高の拡大
ペースが加速しており（図１）、洪水対策の余地が狭まっていることに注意が必要である。 
このところの金融市場では、イラン情勢の長期化によるエネルギーインフレの長期化が意識され
るとともに、 エルニーニョ現象の発生による世界的な食料供給懸念も重なり、 全世界的なインフレ
が警戒されている。こうしたなか、ＦＲ
Ｂ（米連邦準備制度理事会）による利上
げ観測も影響して、世界的に金利に上昇
圧力がかかっている。タイ市場では、中
銀による利下げ実施にもかかわらず、イ
ンフレ顕在化を理由に長期金利は高止ま
りしており （図２） 、 債務負担の増大が懸
念される。タイでは、公的債務の９割近
くを長期債が占めており、短期的な金利
変動の影響を受けにくい構造にある。と
はいえ、コロナ禍を経て財政状況は悪化
している。イラン情勢の悪化、洪水対策など歳出圧力が高まりやすい状況 であるにもかかわらず、
財政面での対応余地が狭まっていることは、 先行きの同国経済にとって足かせとなる可能性がある
点に注意が必要である。                              以 上

Europe Trends

2026 年 10 月７日

1/2 
フランス極右が財政健全化を掲げる狙い 
～見え隠れする政治意図、問われる実現可能性～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト  田中 理 
（℡：050-5474-7494）

（要旨） 
○ 政権獲得を目指すフランスの極右政党・国民連合（ＲＮ）は、2027 年大統領選挙を前に代替
予算案と中期財政計画を発表し、政権発足から 18 ヶ月以内に基礎的財政収支を均衡させ、
2030 年までに財政赤字の対ＧＤＰ比率を３％未満に縮小する計画を明らかにした。 企業減税
や年金負担の軽減を維持しながら、 移民への社会保障給付の抑制、 ＥＵ予算への拠出金削減、
行政機関の統廃合などの歳出削減を計画するが、その多くに法的・政治的な制約がある。 
○ 財政規律を前面に出す背景には、大統領選挙での勝利を睨み、産業界や金融市場の警戒を和
らげ、政権担当能力をアピールする狙いがある。仮に政権を獲得した場合、財政計画の実効
性が国債市場から厳しく問われ、政策の軌道修正を迫られる可能性がある。

2027 年春のフランス大統領選挙での勝利を目指す極右政党・国民連合（ＲＮ）は６日、政府の
2027 年予算案に対する代替予算案と、向こう５年間の中期財政計画を発表した。政権発足から18
ヶ月以内に基礎的財政収支を均衡させ、2030 年までに財政赤字の国内総生産（ＧＤＰ）比を３％
未満、2032 年には2.5％未満に縮小する。公的債務残高のＧＤＰ比を2027 年の 121％前後から、
2032 年には112％に低下させ、財布支出のＧＤＰ比を50％未満に抑える計画だ。2032 年までに
2026 年対比で1400 億ユーロ規模の財政赤字の削減を目指すとしている。さらに、財政赤字を債務
比率の安定に必要な水準に抑えるため、独自の財政ルール（黄金律）を憲法に盛り込み、政権獲
得後１年程度を目途に国民投票にかける考えも示した。 
かつて欧州連合（ＥＵ）の財政規律が国家主権を制約すると批判していたＲＮが、財政赤字の
削減や政府債務の安定を政権公約の柱に据えようとしているのは大きな変化と言える。そこには
大統領選挙を睨んでの政治的な意図が見え隠れする。ＲＮのルペン氏は各種の世論調査で初回投
票の支持率で他候補を圧倒的にリードしているが、決選投票での勝利を確実なものにするには、
ＲＮ政権誕生を警戒する有権者にも支持を広げる必要がある。フランスの財政リスクを巡って金
融市場の緊張が高まるなか、健全な財政運営をアピールすることで、政権担当能力への不安を払
拭する狙いもあるのだろう。 
ＲＮ案では、家計や企業への負担を軽減しながら、大規模な歳出削減を進める。年金の支給開

### Page 2

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
2/2 
始年齢の引き下げ、エネルギーや生活必需品の付加価値税（ＶＡＴ）の引き下げ、中小企業や設
備投資への税優遇などを進めるとしており、その財源として、ＥＵへの拠出金の削減、外国人向
けの社会保障給付や医療費の抑制、行政機関の整理や地方政府の効率化などを計画している。問
題はその実現可能性だ。例えば、ＥＵ予算への拠出金は、加盟国政府が自由に決められるもので
はなく、他の加盟国との合意が必要になる。防衛や競争力の強化などでＥＵの役割が拡大するな
か、今後検討が本格化するＥＵの2028～34 年の次期多年度財政枠組みは、史上最高水準の予算規
模となることが見込まれている。フランスが一方的に拠出金を削れば、ＥＵや他の加盟国との衝
突が避けられない。移民関連支出の削減にも限界がある。外国人への社会保障給付を制限する措
置は、フランスの憲法やＥＵ法との整合性が問われる可能性がある。また、行政機関の統廃合や
公務員の削減などは比較的実行しやすい政策と言えるが、想定する規模の財政節減効果が得られ
るかは不透明だ。実際に政権を担当すれば、政策の軌道修正を迫られる可能性が高い。 
ＲＮは近年、企業経営者や金融市場関係者との対話の機会を増やしてきた。今年４月には、Ｒ
Ｎの首相候補であるバルデラ党首とフランス最大の経営者団体の幹部との会談も行われた。ＲＮ
は、企業負担の軽減、規制緩和、行政事務の効率化など、財界が求めてきた政策を積極的に取り
入れている。今回の中期財政計画も、産業界や金融市場を強く意識した内容と言える。ただ、Ｒ
Ｎの経済・財政運営に対する警戒感が払拭されたわけではない。歳出削減が計画通りに進まなか
った場合、最終的に企業増税で穴埋めされるとの懸念もある、仮にＲＮが政権を獲得しても、Ｅ
Ｕ拠出金の削減が加盟国との交渉で難航し、移民関連支出の削減が司法判断などで制約される一
方、減税や年金の負担軽減措置だけが先行すれば、財政再建への信認は急速に失われかねない。 
ＲＮが掲げる財政規律への転換は、 「抗議政党」から「政権を担う責任政党」への脱皮を目指す
動きと位置づけられる。だが、財政再建の成否を左右するのは、支持層への負担増を避けなが
ら、歳出削減を実際に実行できるかだ。政権を獲得すれば、ＥＵとの交渉や司法上の制約、産業
界からの要求、そして国債市場からの圧力に直面する。選挙前に掲げた財政計画をどこまで現実
路線に修正できるかも、ＲＮの政権担当能力を測る重要な試金石となる。

8 ⽉以来海外系统重要性银⾏股价平均下跌 8% ，中国⼤型银⾏上涨约 9% ，年初⾄今回报达
32% 。这⼀分化源于中外财政、利率与信⽤周期的差异。中⾦认为尽管欧美银⾏盈利能⼒

摘要：
8 ⽉以来海外系统重要性银⾏股价平均下跌 8% ，中国⼤型银⾏上涨约 9% ，年初⾄今平均回报达
到 32% ，跑赢海外同业。尽管短期股价表现不⾜以说明⻓期趋势逆转，但其背后的周期性变化
值得关注。
我们认为，中外财政政策、利率和信⽤周期的差异，以及市场对银⾏未来盈利和资产负债表⻛
险的重新定价，使中国银⾏股具备较好的相对⻛险收益特征，我们继续看好中国⼤型银⾏股相
对海外同业的表现。
摘要
财政取向：从需求刺激到修复资产负债表。同样是政府加杠杆，海外财政扩张⾯临通胀和利率
上⾏压⼒，⽽中国财政政策更加注重化解存量⻛险。根据财政部和央⾏数据， 2026 年中国⼴义
政府融资安排中，债务置换和⾦融机构注资合计 3.1 万亿元，占⽐约 22% ，有助于缓释银⾏信⽤

⻛险和资本压⼒。近期房贷贴息和 PSL 降息扩围等政策进⼀步⽀持信贷需求修复。
利率周期：从息差红利到久期⻛险。利率上⾏初期有利于欧美银⾏息差扩张，但随着利率维持
⾼位，债券浮亏和信⽤成本压⼒逐步显现。 2026 年⼆季度末，美国商业银⾏债券未实现损失约
3267 亿美元。相⽐之下，中国利率环境相对平稳，近年来债券投资收益对银⾏盈利形成⽀持，
未来盈利改善则更加依赖净息差企稳和信贷需求恢复。
信贷周期：扩张空间与⻛险后置。当前海外银⾏信贷增⻓和资产质量仍然较强，但⾼利率环境
下，商业地产、私募信贷和 AI 相关⾼杠杆融资⻛险值得关注。中国信贷需求仍然偏弱，但债务置
换和房地产融资政策有助于缓释存量⻛险，未来信贷需求恢复有待进⼀步财政和房地产政策的
⽀持。
盈利与估值：周期错位下的相对收益机会。2026 年上半年，美国、欧元区银⾏业净利润分别同
⽐增⻓约 21% 和 11% ，⾼于中国上市银⾏的 3% ，海外银⾏盈利能⼒仍然较强。但较⾼盈利⽔平
已⼀定程度反映在估值中，中国上市银⾏ A/H 股平均 PB 约 0.6/0.5 倍，低于欧美银⾏。考虑到中

国银⾏盈利有望保持稳定、估值相对较低，叠加保险等⻓期资⾦对⾼股息资产的配置需求，我
们认为中国银⾏股中⻓期具备较好的相对收益机会。
正⽂：全球银⾏股 “ 东升西落 ” 的启⽰
8 ⽉以来，受到⾼利率冲击，海外系统重要性银⾏股价平均下跌 8% ，部分欧洲⼤型银⾏股价从
⾼点回撤幅度达到 20% 左右。相⽐之下，中国⼤型银⾏股价表现出较强韧性， 8 ⽉以来上涨约
9% ，跑赢海外同业，年初⾄今平均回报达到 32% ，同样位居全球同业前列。
尽管短期股价表现不⾜以说明⻓期趋势逆转，但其背后的周期性变化值得关注。同样是政府加
杠杆，为什么海外银⾏股调整，⽽中国银⾏股表现较好？为什么过去⼏年利好欧美银⾏的⾼利
率环境，反⽽开始成为市场担忧的因素？

财政取向：从需求刺激到修复资产负债表
同样是政府加杠杆，中外财政政策对银⾏资产负债表的影响有所不同。近期能源价格上涨进⼀
步推升通胀预期，市场担忧海外经济体财政扩张可能加⼤利率上⾏压⼒。
对于银⾏⽽⾔，⻓端利率虽然能够扩张息差，但也会增加债券投资浮亏损失，也可能通过提⾼
实体经济融资成本增加信⽤⻛险。⽽中国财政扩张的⼀个重要特点，是将相当⼀部分资⾦⽤于
化解存量债务⻛险和补充⾦融机构资本，实现⾦融体系资产负债表修复。
2026 年两会安排政府融资约 14 万亿元（包括债务置换），其中，隐债置换 2 万亿元、新增专项债
中⽤于化债的 8000 亿元，以及⾦融机构注资 3000 亿元，合计 3.1 万亿元，占⼴义融资安排的约

22% 。
具体⽽⾔， 9 ⽉底推出的两项财政⾦融协同政策能够精准⽀持银⾏信贷需求，其中房贷贴息政策
我们测算每年能够⽀持 1.8 万亿元的新增贷款； PSL 降息为政策性银⾏投放 “ 六张⽹ ” 贷款提供了
稳定、更低成本的资⾦来源，我们测算 “ 六张⽹ ” 投资 “ ⼗五五 ” 时期能够累计拉动贷款超过 16 万亿
元（参⻅《房贷贴息和 PSL 降息的⾦融信号》）。

利率周期：从息差红利到久期⻛险
利率上⾏前期，银⾏往往更受益于息差扩张，但利率上⾏中后期，债券浮亏、信⽤成本上升等
弊端逐渐显现。根据 FDIC 数据，截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度末，美国银⾏业可供出售和持有⾄到期债券
的未实现损失合计约 3267 亿美元，占相关证券摊余成本的 5.5% 。 2023 年硅⾕银⾏事件表明，当
⻓期债券浮亏与负债端流动性压⼒叠加时，利率⻛险可能转化为实际损失。
相⽐之下，中国银⾏业处于利率相对平稳的环境。近年来，债券投资及交易相关收益成为中国
上市银⾏⾮息收⼊的重要⽀撑。根据我们对上市银⾏的测算， 2023 年⾄ 2026 年上半年，其他⾮

息收⼊平均每年贡献约 1.7 个百分点的利润增⻓。
当然，随着国内利率降⾄低位，存量债券浮盈的兑现空间受限，未来中国商业银⾏盈利改善更
依赖净息差企稳和信贷需求恢复。

信贷周期：扩张空间和⻛险后置
从信贷脉冲来看，海外银⾏信贷仍处于扩张阶段，⽽中国信贷周期位于历史偏低⽔平。海外信
贷增⻓⼀⽅⾯受到前期降息的⽀持，另⼀⽅⾯也包括通胀上升带来的营运资⾦需求以及和 AI 等新
兴产业投资拉动。相⽐之下，中国信贷需求仍然偏弱，新增融资更多依靠基建、制造业等政策
⽀持领域。
我们认为更值得关注的是信⽤⻛险的情况，⽬前海外银⾏不良贷款指标保持较低⽔平，但⾼利
率环境下三个领域值得关注：融资成本⾼企叠加空置率上升对商业地产贷款形成压⼒；私募信
贷赎回压⼒及银⾏对⾮银⾦融机构的融资敞⼝； AI 相关⾼杠杆项⽬融资可能⾯临回报和再融资压
⼒。
中国⽅⾯，不良贷款率整体保持稳定，债务置换、房地产融资政策有助于缓解⻛险暴露，居⺠
贷款压⼒有所上升但总体可控。我们认为未来中国信贷周期的修复有待于进⼀步财政和房地产
等政策的⽀持，⽽海外信贷周期在⾼利率环境下⾯临更多挑战。

盈利与估值：周期错位下的相对收益机会
从当前盈利看，欧美银⾏受益于较⾼利率环境和资本市场业务改善，同时信贷增速和资产质量
仍然保持较强韧性，基本⾯相⽐中资银⾏仍然具有优势。 2026 年上半年，美国银⾏业净利润同
⽐增⻓约 21% ，欧元区主要银⾏净利润增⻓约 11% ，⽽中国上市银⾏净利润增⻓约 3% ； 2026 年
上半年，美国银⾏ ROE 约 13% ，欧元区主要银⾏约 11% ，呈现上升趋势；中国上市银⾏ ROE 约
10% ，由于盈利增速偏低 ROE 缓慢下降。
但从估值看，海外银⾏较⾼的盈利⽔平也已经⼀定程度反映在股价中。当前中国上市银⾏ A/H 股
平均市净率约为 0.6 和 0.5 倍，⽽欧美银⾏普遍在 0.8 倍以上，部分⼤型银⾏已经达到 1.5-3.0 倍。
从市净率 -ROE 的相关性分析来看，部分中资银⾏估值已出现⼀定吸引⼒。
向前看，我们认为中国上市银⾏未来 1-2 年净利润增速有望保持稳定，如果财政刺激能够进⼀步

推动信贷需求恢复，银⾏利润增速存在改善空间。相⽐之下，欧美银⾏仍具有较⾼的盈利能
⼒，但在⾼利率环境可能⾯临更多不确定性。
考虑到中国⼤型银⾏较低的估值、稳定的分红能⼒，以及保险等⻓期配置型资⾦对⾼股息资产
的持续需求，我们认为中国银⾏股中⻓期相对海外银⾏具备较好的相对⻛险收益特征。
